 \int_{Y_t\cap\text{chart}}|\eta_t|^2=o(s^d)
 \quad\text{if }F(o)=0.$$

Properness gives finitely many such charts covering the central fiber and, after shrinking the base, all nearby fibers. If there were no maximal crossings, every chart would contribute $O(s^{d-1})$. If maximal crossings existed but all their coefficients vanished, their contributions would be $o(s^d)$ by <a href="#eq:family-zero-residue" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-zero-residue">[eq:family-zero-residue]</a>, with all other contributions again $O(s^{d-1})$. Either conclusion contradicts the lower bound in <a href="#eq:family-volume-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-volume-growth">[eq:family-volume-growth]</a>. At least one maximal crossing therefore has nonzero coefficient. This proves <a href="#eq:family-maximal-residue" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-maximal-residue">[eq:family-maximal-residue]</a>. At that point the functions $z_1,\ldots,z_d$ may be chosen to be algebraic local defining functions of the corresponding components; the unit in the product equation is absorbed in $z_0$. They are thus restrictions of rational functions on the algebraic total space, as required for the intersection estimates in Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">4</a>. This completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:family">[prop:family]</a>.

<a id="stmt-cor-family-torus"></a>
<div id="cor:family-torus" class="corollary">

**Corollary 14**. *Fix an irrational $\lambda$ and choose the lifted path in Proposition <a href="#prop:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:family">[prop:family]</a> with $\tau=\lambda+iy$. For every sufficiently large $y$, the corresponding smooth fiber contains an embedded special Lagrangian real $d$-torus for its Ricci-flat metric in the polarization class. This torus is isotopic to a coordinate angle torus in the chart <a href="#eq:family-maximal-residue" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-maximal-residue">[eq:family-maximal-residue]</a>. The period and monodromy on that fiber are the exact marked data <a href="#eq:family-period" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-period">[eq:family-period]</a>–<a href="#eq:family-monodromy-image" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:family-monodromy-image">[eq:family-monodromy-image]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-family-torus"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-family-torus</summary>

*Proof.* The algebraicity, projectivity, extension of the polarization, volume growth, logarithmic extension, and nonzero coefficient required by Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">4</a> are all supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:family">[prop:family]</a>. The theorem applies to every sufficiently small parameter, in particular along the chosen path, where $\operatorname{Re}\tau=\lambda$ and the marking is already fixed. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:rotation"></a>
### Rotation and return to the original manifold

We now complete the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The marking in Proposition <a href="#prop:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:family">[prop:family]</a> will be used throughout: all second cohomology groups are identified with the lattice of the differentiable manifold underlying the given $X$, and $q$ denotes both the quadratic form and its associated symmetric bilinear form.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### The primitive class and the chosen fiber

<a id="eq:rotation-original-sign"></a>
Recall the primitive class $e$ and line bundle $E$ constructed at the start of Section <a href="#sec:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:family">4</a>. Write $E_X=E$, so that $L\simeq E_X^{\otimes m_0}$. The class $e$ is nonzero, nef, and $q(e)=0$; its sign was fixed so that, for every Kähler class $\kappa$ on $X$, $$q(e,\kappa)>0.
\label{eq:rotation-original-sign}$$

<a id="eq:rotation-monodromy-image"></a>
Fix $\lambda\in\mathbb R\setminus\mathbb Q$ and use the family of Proposition <a href="#prop:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:family">[prop:family]</a>. Its rational classes $e,f,v,h$ satisfy $$q(e)=q(f)=0,\quad q(e,f)=1,\quad
 q(e,v)=q(f,v)=0,\quad a:=q(v)>0,$$ and $h\perp P:=\langle e,f,v\rangle_{\mathbb Q}$. The normalized period and the local monodromy $M$ on $H^2$ are <a id="eq:rotation-period"></a>$$p(\tau)=f+\tau v-\frac a2\tau^2e,
       \qquad\tau=\lambda+iy,\quad y>0,
\label{eq:rotation-period}$$ <a id="eq:rotation-monodromy"></a>$$Me=e,\qquad Mv=v-aw e,\qquad
 Mf=f+w v-\frac{aw^2}{2}e,\quad w\ne0.
\label{eq:rotation-monodromy}$$ Consequently, $$(M-1)P=\langle e,v\rangle_{\mathbb Q}.
\label{eq:rotation-monodromy-image}$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">4</a>, applied along the prescribed vertical ray, provides a smooth fiber $Y=Y_t$, its polarized Ricci-flat metric $g$, and an embedded special Lagrangian torus $T\subset Y$. Here $\dim_{\mathbb C}Y=2n$, $\dim_{\mathbb R}T=2n$, and $T$ is isotopic to a coordinate torus in a semistable chart $z_0z_1\cdots z_{2n}=t$. A constant rescaling of $g$, as used in the torus construction, does not affect these properties.

<a id="eq:rotation-k-class"></a>
Write the present complex structure as $I$. Choose a hyperkähler triple $(I,J,K)$ for $g$, with $IJ=K$, so that <a id="eq:rotation-normalization"></a>$$\omega_J+i\omega_K=c\sigma_t,\qquad c>0,
       \qquad [\omega_I]\in\mathbb R_{>0}h.
\label{eq:rotation-normalization}$$ The positive scalar normalizes the length of $\sigma_t$; its given phase fixes the ordered pair $(J,K)$. Since $[\sigma_t]=p(\tau)$, $$=cy(v-a\lambda e),\qquad
 q(e,[\omega_J])=c>0.
\label{eq:rotation-k-class}$$ The second equality follows from $q(e,p(\tau))=1$ and will retain the required sign of $e$ after rotation.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Restriction, calibration, and complex rotation

The following criterion isolates the rotation argument. Cohomological vanishing is enough because the special Lagrangian condition turns volume into a period.

<a id="stmt-lem-rotation-criterion"></a>
<div id="lem:rotation-criterion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (A cohomological rotation criterion). *Let $(Y,g,I,J,K)$ be a hyperkähler manifold of real dimension $4n$, where $n\ge1$ and $IJ=K$, and let $i:T\hookrightarrow Y$ be a compact, connected, oriented embedded special Lagrangian submanifold for the complex structure $I$. If $i^*[\omega_K]=0$ in $H^2(T,\mathbb R)$, then $T$ is a complex $n$-dimensional submanifold for $J$ and is Lagrangian for $\omega_K+i\omega_I$. If $T$ is diffeomorphic to a real $2n$-torus, then it is a complex torus for $J$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rotation-criterion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rotation-criterion</summary>

<a id="eq:rotation-wirtinger"></a>
*Proof.* The normalized parallel holomorphic volume form for $(Y,I,g)$ is $$\Omega_I=\frac{(\omega_J+i\omega_K)^n}{n!}.$$ To check the normalization, in a symplectic unitary frame one has $\omega_J+i\omega_K=\sum_{j=1}^n dz_{2j-1}\wedge dz_{2j}$; its $n$th power divided by $n!$ is the unit holomorphic volume form. It has absolute value one on an orthonormal frame of any $I$-Lagrangian real $2n$-plane. The special Lagrangian calibration identity (Harvey and Lawson 1982, III.1, Theorem 1.10 and Corollary 1.11) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[HarveyLawson](#ref-HarveyLawson)] therefore gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g(T)=\left|\int_T\Omega_I\right|.$$ All terms containing $\omega_K$ in this integral vanish: the hypothesis makes $i^*\omega_K$ exact, while all the factors in the expansion are closed. Stokes’ theorem then yields $$\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g(T)
 =\left|\int_T\frac{\omega_J^n}{n!}\right|
 \le\int_T\left|\frac{i^*\omega_J^n}{n!}\right|
 \le\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g(T).
\label{eq:rotation-wirtinger}$$ The last inequality is the pointwise Wirtinger inequality (Harvey and Lawson 1982, II.6, Theorem 6.11) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[HarveyLawson](#ref-HarveyLawson)]. Its continuous, nonnegative deficit has integral zero, so equality holds at every point of $T$. Equality planes are exactly the $J$-invariant real $2n$-planes. Thus $T$ is a complex $n$-dimensional submanifold of $$Y'=(Y,J).$$ Its orientation may now be chosen to be the complex orientation; the absolute period in <a href="#eq:rotation-wirtinger" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-wirtinger">[eq:rotation-wirtinger]</a> made the initial choice immaterial.

For $J$ the holomorphic symplectic form is $\sigma_J=\omega_K+i\omega_I$. We already have $\omega_I|_T=0$ pointwise. Since $JT_xT=T_xT$, for $u,v\in T_xT$ the quaternion identities give $$\omega_K(u,v)=g(IJu,v)=\omega_I(Ju,v)=0.$$ Hence $\sigma_J|_T=0$: the rotated submanifold is holomorphically Lagrangian.

Suppose now that $T$ is diffeomorphic to a real $2n$-torus. The restriction of $\omega_J$ makes $T$ Kähler. Consequently $b_1(T)=2n$ and $h^{1,0}(T)=n$. Its Albanese map $$\alpha:T\longrightarrow\operatorname{Alb}(T)$$ has a complex $n$-torus as target and induces an isomorphism on integral first cohomology. Both integral cohomology rings are exterior algebras on first cohomology, so $\alpha^*$ is an isomorphism in every degree. In particular, $\alpha$ has nonzero degree; holomorphicity makes that degree positive, hence equal to one, and $\alpha$ is surjective.

Choose a Kähler class $\xi$ on $T$. Surjectivity on $H^2(-,\mathbb R)$ writes $\xi=\alpha^*\beta$ for some real class $\beta$ on $\operatorname{Alb}(T)$. If a fiber contained a positive-dimensional irreducible compact analytic subvariety $V$ of dimension $k$, then $$\int_V\xi^k=0,$$ since $\alpha|_V$ is constant. This contradicts Kähler positivity. Thus $\alpha$ is proper with finite fibers, hence finite. A finite degree-one holomorphic map to a normal complex space is an isomorphism. This proves that $T$ is a holomorphic Lagrangian complex torus in $Y'$. ◻

</details>

We verify the criterion for the torus constructed above, using its coordinate isotopy class. This is where the monodromy information is needed.

For a coordinate torus $T_0=(\mathbb R/2\pi\mathbb Z)^{2n}$ choose its fixed radii $r_1,\ldots,r_{2n}$. Around the circle $t(\theta)=t\exp(i\theta)$, the formula $$z_j=r_j\exp(iy_j)\quad(1\le j\le2n),\qquad
 z_0=\frac{t\exp(i\theta)}{z_1\cdots z_{2n}}$$ defines a family of embeddings of this same parameterized $T_0$. All absolute values remain fixed, so the family stays inside the chart, and the embeddings at $\theta=0$ and $2\pi$ agree as parameterized maps. The induced restriction maps form a morphism from the fiber cohomology local system to the constant local system $H^k(T_0,\mathbb R)$. Therefore, in every degree $k$, <a id="eq:rotation-restriction-monodromy"></a>$$i_0^*M_k=i_0^*:H^k(Y,\mathbb R)\longrightarrow H^k(T_0,\mathbb R),
\label{eq:rotation-restriction-monodromy}$$ where $M_k$ is monodromy in degree $k$. This statement controls the restriction of classes in degree two, as well as the torus’s top-degree period. Isotopy identifies the restriction maps for $T_0$ and $T$. Thus, for $i:T\hookrightarrow Y$, $i^*(M-1)=0$ on $H^2(Y,\mathbb R)$. Equations <a href="#eq:rotation-monodromy-image" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-monodromy-image">[eq:rotation-monodromy-image]</a> and <a href="#eq:rotation-k-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-k-class">[eq:rotation-k-class]</a> give <a id="eq:rotation-k-vanishing"></a>$$i^*[\omega_K]=0.
\label{eq:rotation-k-vanishing}$$ Replacing monodromy by its inverse or a positive power preserves the image in <a href="#eq:rotation-monodromy-image" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-monodromy-image">[eq:rotation-monodromy-image]</a>, so the conclusion is independent of this convention and of the finite base changes.

Lemma <a href="#lem:rotation-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rotation-criterion">15</a> now makes $T$ a holomorphic Lagrangian complex torus on the rotated manifold $Y'=(Y,J)$.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The rational isotropic ray after rotation

<a id="eq:rotation-j-period"></a>
The period plane of $Y'$ is $$\langle[\omega_K],[\omega_I]\rangle_{\mathbb R}
       =\langle v-a\lambda e,h\rangle_{\mathbb R}.
\label{eq:rotation-j-period}$$ It is perpendicular to $e$, so $e$ is an integral $(1,1)$ class on $Y'$; see Figure <a href="#fig:rotation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:rotation">1</a>.

<figure id="fig:rotation">

<figcaption>The period planes before and after rotation, inside the positive three-space spanned by the hyperkähler forms. The class $e$ is perpendicular to $[ω_{I}]$ and $[ω_{K}]$, so it becomes a $(1,1)$ class for $J$; its pairing with $[ω_{J}]$ fixes the positive sign.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-rotation"></a>

  \coordinate (K) at (0,2.65);
  \fill[blue!9] (O)--(-2.35,-.705)--(-2.35,1.35)--(0,2.055)--cycle;
  \fill[orange!14] (O)--(2.65,-.492)--(2.65,1.563)--(0,2.055)--cycle;
  \draw[->,semithick] (O)--(I) node[right,align=left] {$[\omega_I]$\\$\in\R_{>0}h$};

Visual witness description of PDF page 27 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Three vectors emanate from a shared base point: a vertical arrow pointing straight up labeled $[\omega_K] \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}(v - a\lambda e)$, an arrow pointing down-left labeled $[\omega_I]$, and an arrow pointing down-right labeled $[\omega_J] \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}h$. Two shaded planar regions are shown sharing the vertical edge: a blue shaded quadrilateral on the left labeled "period plane for $I$", and a tan/orange shaded quadrilateral on the right labeled "period plane for $J$".
```


<a id="eq:rotation-rational-orthogonality"></a>
Let $x\in H^{1,1}(Y')\cap H^2(Y',\mathbb Q)$. Orthogonality to the period plane says $$q(x,h)=0,\qquad q(x,v)=a\lambda q(x,e).$$ The three numbers $q(x,v)$, $a$, and $q(x,e)$ are rational, whereas $a\ne0$ and $\lambda\notin\mathbb Q$. Therefore $$q(x,e)=q(x,v)=0.
\label{eq:rotation-rational-orthogonality}$$

<a id="eq:rotation-unique-ray"></a>
The restriction of $q$ to $H^{1,1}(Y',\mathbb R)$ has signature $(1,b_2-3)$. In a Lorentzian space, the orthogonal complement of a nonzero null vector is negative semidefinite with radical its own line. Applying this to $e$ and <a href="#eq:rotation-rational-orthogonality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-rational-orthogonality">[eq:rotation-rational-orthogonality]</a> proves $$\begin{gathered}
 q(x)\le0\quad\text{for }x\in H^{1,1}(Y')\cap H^2(Y',\mathbb Q),\\
 q(x)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x\in\mathbb Qe.
 \end{gathered}
\label{eq:rotation-unique-ray}$$ In particular, $Y'$ is nonprojective: a projective hyperkähler manifold has an integral ample class of positive BBF square. This is the necessary direction of the projectivity criterion (Huybrechts 1999, 2003) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[Huybrechts](#ref-Huybrechts), [HuybrechtsErratum](#ref-HuybrechtsErratum)]. The rational Picard space may contain negative-square classes; its isotropic classes are exactly those on $\mathbb Qe$. Equation <a href="#eq:rotation-k-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-k-class">[eq:rotation-k-class]</a> places $e$ in the closure of the positive cone containing the Kähler class $[\omega_J]$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### A semiample point and deformation back to $X$

We state explicitly the two external results used in this step.

<a id="stmt-thm-nonprojective-torus"></a>
<div id="thm:nonprojective-torus" class="theorem">

**Theorem 16** (Greb–Lehn–Rollenske (Greb, Lehn, and Rollenske 2013, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[GLR](#ref-GLR)]). *If a nonprojective compact irreducible hyperkähler manifold $Z$ of complex dimension $2n$ contains a holomorphic Lagrangian complex torus $S$, then its algebraic dimension is $n$ and it has a holomorphic algebraic reduction $Z\to B$ which is a Lagrangian fibration with $S$ as a fiber. In particular, this fibration has normal projective base and provides a nontrivial semiample pullback bundle on $Z$.*

</div>

The projectivity assertion follows from the line-bundle construction of the algebraic reduction in (Greb, Lehn, and Rollenske 2013, sec. 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[GLR](#ref-GLR)] and Stein factorization.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### The fixed-class deformation theorem.

Let $N$ be a compact simply connected differentiable manifold of hyperkähler type with maximal holonomy, let $\mathcal T^\circ(N)$ be one connected component of its Teichmüller space, and fix a nonzero primitive integral class $e$ with $q(e)=0$. Write $\mathcal C_I$ for the component of the positive cone containing the Kähler cone of $(N,I)$, and set $$\mathcal T^\circ_e
 =\{[I]\in\mathcal T^\circ(N):
       e\in H^{1,1}(N,I),\ e\in\overline{\mathcal C_I}\}.$$ This is the positive-sign locus of (Soldatenkov and Verbitsky 2025, Definition 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[SV](#ref-SV)]. By (Soldatenkov and Verbitsky 2025, Proposition 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[SV](#ref-SV)] it is connected. Let $E_I$ denote the unique holomorphic bundle with class $e$. Define $\mathcal T^\circ_{e,\mathrm{nef}}$ and $\mathcal T^\circ_{e,\mathrm{sa}}$ by requiring $E_I$ to be nef and semiample, respectively.

<a id="stmt-thm-fixed-class"></a>
<div id="thm:fixed-class" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:rotation-sv"></a>
**Theorem 17** (Soldatenkov–Verbitsky (Soldatenkov and Verbitsky 2025, Theorem 3.7(v)) <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[SV](#ref-SV)]). *With this notation, $$\mathcal T^\circ_{e,\mathrm{sa}}\ne\varnothing
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal T^\circ_{e,\mathrm{nef}}
      =\mathcal T^\circ_{e,\mathrm{sa}}.
\label{eq:rotation-sv}$$*

</div>

Its family formulation, (Soldatenkov and Verbitsky 2025, Theorem 3.8) <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[SV](#ref-SV)], says that a nef isotropic bundle is semiample when its pair deforms, in a smooth connected family carrying a line bundle, to a semiample pair. We use <a href="#eq:rotation-sv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-sv">[eq:rotation-sv]</a> directly.

Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:nonprojective-torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:nonprojective-torus">16</a> to $(Y',T)$. Choose an ample bundle $H$ on its fibration base and let $A$ be its pullback to $Y'$. The bundle $A$ is semiample and nef, and its first Chern class $b$ is nonzero: a semipositive representative of a generated power has positive trace on an open set where the map has positive rank. Since the base has dimension $n$, $\int_{Y'}b^{2n}=0$. Fujiki’s relation (Fujiki 1987) <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[Fujiki](#ref-Fujiki)] gives $q(b)=0$. Equation <a href="#eq:rotation-unique-ray" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-unique-ray">[eq:rotation-unique-ray]</a> and the primitivity of $e$ now give $$b=k e,\qquad k\in\mathbb Z.$$ Both $b$ and $e$ pair positively with $[\omega_J]$: for $b$ this is the nonzero nef null-vector property, and for $e$ it is <a href="#eq:rotation-k-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-k-class">[eq:rotation-k-class]</a>. Hence $k>0$.

Let $E_{Y'}$ be the unique bundle on $Y'$ with first Chern class $e$. Injectivity of the first Chern class map gives $$A\simeq E_{Y'}^{\otimes k}.$$ A globally generated positive power of $A$ is therefore a globally generated positive power of $E_{Y'}$. Thus the *specified primitive class* $e$ is semiample on $Y'$.

The marking furnished by Proposition <a href="#prop:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:family">[prop:family]</a> identifies $Y$ with a point of the same ambient component $\mathcal T^\circ(N)$ as the original $X$. The hyperkähler twistor sphere connects $I$ to $J$ on that same marked differentiable manifold, so $Y'$ also belongs to this component. On $X$, $e$ is of type $(1,1)$ and has the positive sign by <a href="#eq:rotation-original-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-original-sign">[eq:rotation-original-sign]</a>. On $Y'$ the corresponding statements follow from <a href="#eq:rotation-j-period" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-j-period">[eq:rotation-j-period]</a> and <a href="#eq:rotation-k-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-k-class">[eq:rotation-k-class]</a>. Consequently both endpoints lie in the same positive fixed-class locus $\mathcal T^\circ_e$. The semiample point $Y'$ makes its semiample locus nonempty; the original point $X$ belongs to its nef locus by hypothesis. Equation <a href="#eq:rotation-sv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-sv">[eq:rotation-sv]</a> now proves that $E_X$ is semiample on $X$.

Choose $s>0$ such that $E_X^{\otimes s}$ is globally generated. Then $L^{\otimes s}\simeq(E_X^{\otimes s})^{\otimes m_0}$ is globally generated as well. In particular, on the original manifold the evaluation morphism $$H^0(X,L^{\otimes s})\otimes_{\mathbb C}\mathcal O_X
                    \longrightarrow L^{\otimes s}$$ is surjective everywhere. This proves the semiampleness assertion of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The fibration defined by a generated power

<a id="eq:rotation-ample-pullback"></a>
We derive the fibration description from semiampleness, using the Fujiki argument for Lagrangian fibers of Matsushita (Matsushita 1999, 2001) <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[Matsushita1999](#ref-Matsushita1999), [MatsushitaAddendum](#ref-MatsushitaAddendum)]. Let $m>0$ make $L^{\otimes m}$ globally generated, let $$g:X\longrightarrow Z\subset\mathbb P H^0(X,L^{\otimes m})^*$$ be its morphism to the image, and take its Stein factorization $g=\nu\circ f$: $$X\xrightarrow{\ f\ }B\xrightarrow{\ \nu\ }Z.$$ The space $B$ is normal, $f$ has connected fibers, and $\nu$ is finite. Since $Z$ is projective, $B$ is projective and $H:=\nu^*\mathcal O_Z(1)$ is ample. By construction $$L^{\otimes m}\simeq f^*H.
\label{eq:rotation-ample-pullback}$$

<a id="eq:rotation-base-dimension"></a>
Put $\ell=c_1(L)$ and choose a Kähler class $\kappa$ on $X$. The positive sign gives $q(\ell,\kappa)>0$. Fujiki’s formula and $q(\ell)=0$ give the polynomial identity <a id="eq:rotation-numerical-dimension"></a>$$\int_X(\kappa+t\ell)^{2n}
       =c_X\bigl(q(\kappa)+2tq(\kappa,\ell)\bigr)^n,
       \qquad c_X>0.
\label{eq:rotation-numerical-dimension}$$ Comparison of coefficients shows $$\int_X\ell^k\kappa^{2n-k}
 \begin{cases}
 >0,&0\le k\le n,\\
 =0,&n<k\le2n.
 \end{cases}$$ On the other hand, a pullback of the Fubini–Study form by $g$ represents $m\ell$, is semipositive, and has generic rank $\dim Z=\dim B$. Its $k$th power wedged with a Kähler form has positive integral precisely for $k\le\dim B$. Thus $$\dim_{\mathbb C}B=n.
\label{eq:rotation-base-dimension}$$

Let $F$ be a general smooth fiber. Its Poincaré dual class is a positive multiple of $\ell^n$. Indeed, choose a very ample power $H^{\otimes a_0}$ and intersect $n$ general members of its linear system on $B$. Their intersection is a nonempty finite set of transverse points in the smooth regular-value locus of $f$. Pulling back gives the union of the corresponding smooth fibers and represents $(a_0m)^n\ell^n$. These fibers have the same homology class, by smooth proper transport along the connected regular-value locus. This proves the claim.

<a id="eq:rotation-fiber-vanishing"></a>
For $n\ge2$, polarization of Fujiki’s relation gives $$\int_X\ell^n\kappa^{n-2}\sigma\overline\sigma=0.
\label{eq:rotation-fiber-vanishing}$$ To see the vanishing directly, each summand in the polarized formula is a product of pairings of the $2n$ degree-two factors. Every copy of $\ell$ is orthogonal to $\ell$, $\sigma$, and $\overline\sigma$, so it would have to pair with a copy of $\kappa$. There are $n$ copies of $\ell$ and only $n-2$ copies of $\kappa$, and therefore every summand is zero. Using the fiber class, <a href="#eq:rotation-fiber-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-fiber-vanishing">[eq:rotation-fiber-vanishing]</a> gives $$\int_F\sigma|_F\wedge\overline{\sigma|_F}
                         \wedge\kappa|_F^{\,n-2}=0.$$ In unitary coordinates this integrand is a fixed positive constant times the squared pointwise norm of $\sigma|_F$ times the Kähler volume form. Hence $\sigma|_F=0$. For $n=1$, the restriction of a holomorphic two-form to the curve $F$ vanishes automatically. The fibers in question have complex dimension $n$ by <a href="#eq:rotation-base-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-base-dimension">[eq:rotation-base-dimension]</a>, so they are Lagrangian.

The same assertion holds for every irreducible component of every fiber by Matsushita’s equidimensionality theorem (Matsushita 2000, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-64"></a>[[MatsushitaEquidimensional](#ref-MatsushitaEquidimensional)]. Its hypotheses are exactly those now established: $X$ is Kähler, $f$ is proper and surjective with normal base, and a general smooth fiber is Lagrangian. The holomorphic symplectic form is closed, as every holomorphic form on a compact Kähler manifold is. Thus every fiber component has dimension $n$ and is isotropic on its smooth locus. This also gives the Lagrangian fibration used in Section <a href="#sec:finiteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finiteness">6</a>.

Conversely, an ample bundle has a globally generated positive power, so <a href="#eq:rotation-ample-pullback" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-ample-pullback">[eq:rotation-ample-pullback]</a> implies semiampleness of $L$. This proves the equivalence between semiampleness and the fibration description in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="subsec:projective-base"></a>
#### Projective-space bases

We finish with the additional conclusion for projective $X$ in Corollary <a href="#cor:projective-base" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:projective-base">3</a>.

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-fixed-class"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-fixed-class</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a very ample line bundle $H$ on $X$, independently of $L$, with embedding $i:X\hookrightarrow\mathbb P^N$; the closed graph embedding $(f,i):X\hookrightarrow B\times\mathbb P^N$ exhibits $H$ as relatively very ample for $f$, so $f$ is projective. The remaining hypotheses are supplied by Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>; hence the companion’s Theorem 1.1 (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-65"></a>[[ProjectiveSpaceBases2026](#ref-ProjectiveSpaceBases2026)] identifies $B$ with $\mathbb P^n_{\mathbb C}$, and the following no-multiple-fiber paragraph there gives the divisor assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:finiteness"></a>
### Finiteness of smooth deformation types

We now deduce Corollary <a href="#cor:finiteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:finiteness">2</a>. The reduction from an arbitrary manifold with $b_2\ge5$ to one with a nef isotropic class is the argument of Sawon (Sawon 2003, Proposition 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[Sawon](#ref-Sawon)]; we give it here to show precisely where the second-Betti-number hypothesis enters. Here deformation equivalence is generated by smooth proper families of compact irreducible holomorphic symplectic Kähler manifolds over connected complex bases. The dimension and second Betti number are constant in such a family.

We use the following smooth consequence of Engel–Filipazzi–Greer–Mauri–Svaldi (Engel et al. 2025, Theorem B and Corollary A.3) <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[EFGMS](#ref-EFGMS)]: in fixed dimension, compact irreducible holomorphic symplectic Kähler manifolds admitting a Lagrangian fibration have only finitely many smooth deformation types. Indeed, their Corollary A.3 deforms such a fibration to a projective one, and Theorem B gives finiteness for the projective representatives. The deformations in these results are locally trivial, meaning that each point has an analytic neighborhood which deforms as a product. They therefore preserve smoothness and give ordinary smooth deformations for the manifolds considered here.

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-fixed-class-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-fixed-class</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:finiteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:finiteness">2</a>.* Let $X$ have complex dimension $2n$ and $r=b_2(X)\ge5$. Its rational BBF form is nondegenerate and indefinite, of signature $(3,r-3)$. Meyer’s theorem says that an indefinite rational quadratic form in at least five variables represents zero nontrivially (Serre 1973, vol. 7, chap. IV, §3.2, Corollary 2) <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[SerreArithmetic](#ref-SerreArithmetic)]. Clearing denominators and dividing by the positive divisibility supplies a primitive integral vector $$0\ne e\in H^2(X,\mathbb Z),\qquad q(e)=0.$$

Consider periods perpendicular to $e$ in the marked deformation component of $X$. The quotient $e^\perp/\mathbb Re$ has signature $(2,r-4)$, so this period locus is nonempty. A generic period in it is perpendicular to no integral vector outside $\mathbb Qe$: each such additional orthogonality condition is a proper hyperplane section, and there are countably many of them. Period surjectivity therefore gives a deformation $X_0$ of $X$ with $$\mathop{\mathrm{NS}}(X_0)=\mathbb Ze.$$ There are no negative integral $(1,1)$ classes on $X_0$. The Kähler-cone description recalled in Section <a href="#sec:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:family">4</a> consequently identifies its Kähler cone with its positive cone. One of $e,-e$ lies in the closure of that cone; the corresponding unique line bundle is nonzero, nef, and isotropic.

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> makes this bundle semiample and gives a holomorphic Lagrangian fibration on $X_0$. Thus every smooth deformation type under consideration contains a manifold with such a fibration. The smooth consequence of (Engel et al. 2025, Theorem B and Corollary A.3) <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[EFGMS](#ref-EFGMS)] stated above leaves only finitely many possibilities in dimension $2n$. ◻

</details>

The restriction $b_2\ge5$ enters only through Meyer’s theorem. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> itself has no such restriction, since its isotropic class is part of the hypothesis.
